Kyousuke is therefore attempting to process at once what other siblings may negotiate gradually.

Kōhei’s diagnosis is essentially:

> **Their relationship has changed too rapidly for Kyousuke to understand what stage it has reached.**

This explanation neither normalizes nor automatically romanticizes the bond.

It places the instability in disrupted development.

Their estrangement prevented ordinary differentiation.

Their sudden reunion compressed intimacy and separation anxiety into the same period.

---

# 13\. The sibling-cuteness contest

Kyousuke and Kōhei compete over whose sister is cuter.

The scene is broad comedy, but it reveals how Kyousuke now understands Kirino’s appeal.

He claims:

> **「妹ってのはな。兄貴と一緒にいるときにこそ、最高の魅力を発揮するんだよ！」**

A little sister shows her greatest charm when she is with her older brother.

Kyousuke does not use only professional model photographs. He uses the purikura from their fake date.

The image that proves Kirino’s cuteness is relational.

She is most meaningful not as:

-   public model;

-   athlete;

-   author;

-   or isolated beautiful girl,


but as the person produced when she and Kyousuke occupy the same frame.

## The purikura remains unstable

Kyousuke has reclassified the fake-date photograph as an image of sibling intimacy.

But its visual language remains romantic:

-   hearts;

-   couple poses;

-   first-name closeness;

-   and shared performance.


He uses a romantic artifact to prove a familial claim.

This perfectly summarizes the state of the central relationship after Volume 8.

---

# 14\. Kirino cooperates in the sibling performance

When Kyousuke asks for a cute photograph, Kirino responds verbally with abuse but sends a carefully posed image.

Later, she asks whether he won the contest and becomes irritated by Sena’s cheek kiss.

This demonstrates that she is not merely victimized by Kyousuke’s siscon display.

She participates in it.

She wants:

-   to be praised;

-   to defeat another sister;

-   to be visibly special to Kyousuke;

-   and to control the boundaries of physical affection.


She will provide a cute self-portrait.

She will not give him the kiss another sister gave her brother.

The distinction indicates that she wants emotional confirmation while remaining frightened by actions that would make the comparison too direct.

---

# 15\. *Chameleon Daughter*: Saori’s unstable pronouns

Saori’s chapter opens with a linguistic problem.

Which first-person pronoun should she use?

-   **私**

-   **わたし**

-   **我輩**

-   **拙者**


Each pronoun belongs to a different Saori.

She observes that, after using so many:

-   names;

-   appearances;

-   speech patterns;

-   and social personae,


the concept of a single **素の自分**—an unperformed natural self—has begun to lose meaning.

This is the most explicit theoretical statement about identity in the series so far.

> **Saori does not have a false persona covering one true self. She has multiple authored selves that have become real through repeated use and relational consequence.**

---

# 16\. The child beneath Saori Bajeena

At twelve, Saori is:

-   unusually tall;

-   physically conspicuous;

-   shy;

-   socially anxious;

-   frequently absent from school;

-   and acutely aware of how other people evaluate her.


Her current ability to read a room begins in fear.

She learned to monitor:

-   facial expressions;

-   status;

-   conversational rhythm;

-   hostility;

-   and exclusion


because she feared making a social mistake.

The skill that later lets her mediate others was first a defensive adaptation.

This is similar to Kyousuke’s humor and Kirino’s aggression:

> A protective mechanism becomes a socially productive identity.

---

# 17\. Kaori and Pretty Garden

Saori’s older sister Kaori introduces her to an otaku circle called **Pretty Garden**.

Kaori is:

-   charismatic;

-   competent;

-   socially fearless;

-   interested in many pursuits;

-   and determined to experience everything she desires.


Her maxim is effectively:

> **「わたしがやりたいのは全部なんだ」**

She wants all of it.

In this sense, Kaori anticipates Kirino’s integrated-self philosophy.

She refuses the notion that a person must have only one legitimate:

-   interest;

-   career;

-   community;

-   or identity.


## Saori learns community by watching it

Pretty Garden contains:

-   creators;

-   former chūnibyō adults;

-   collectors;

-   specialists;

-   and people using several names and personae.


Saori initially watches rather than participates.

She learns:

-   how groups generate rhythm;

-   how eccentric people remain connected;

-   how hospitality matters;

-   how objects become shared reference points;

-   and how a charismatic center can make difference feel coherent.


The tragedy is that she does not realize the group is temporary.

---

# 18\. Pretty Garden dissolves

The circle’s members gradually leave because of:

-   work;

-   relationships;

-   education;

-   relocation;

-   and changing priorities.


Nobody necessarily betrays Saori.

Life differentiates them.

But from her perspective, the result is abandonment.

The physical collection remains while the social world disappears.

This explains why Saori reacted so strongly to:

-   Kirino’s departure;

-   Kuroneko’s move;

-   the romantic triangle;

-   and being excluded from plans that threatened group continuity.


She is not merely an extrovert who enjoys parties.

She is attempting to prevent the repetition of a formative loss.

---

# 19\. Saori Bajeena is an entire community compressed into one person

Kanata, one of Pretty Garden’s creators, passes the swirl glasses to Saori and calls them magical glasses capable of changing a life.

Saori later explains:

> **「約十人の男女で構成されていた『小さな庭園』というオタクサークルを、私一人で再現しようとしたものが『沙織・バジーナ』なのです。」**

Saori Bajeena is her attempt to reproduce, alone, an entire circle of roughly ten people. ÄåüEæ}èGòt)(Æ╟ë┴ìZÉ│12-05-24)-16

This is the volume’s deepest account of performed identity.

Saori Bajeena incorporates:

-   Kaori’s charisma;

-   Kanata’s comic otaku speech;

-   other members’ aesthetic knowledge;

-   the old group’s hospitality;

-   its social energy;

-   and Saori’s own determination never to let a community disappear again.


She is not merely impersonating one admired person.

She is performing a social archive.

## Persona as memorial labor

Saori’s identity is a form of memory-work.

Every time she:

-   arranges an outing;

-   makes someone laugh;

-   introduces two lonely people;

-   or sustains a group conversation,


she reenacts the world she lost.

Her glasses are not simply concealment.

They are a memorial object and an instrument of institution-building.

---

# 20\. Class privilege and social infrastructure

Saori’s family wealth explains aspects of her social power that previously seemed almost magical.

She has access to:

-   extensive space;

-   rare collections;

-   technology;

-   transportation;

-   industry contacts;

-   and the financial capacity to support hobbies.


Her ability to build community is genuine labor, but that labor is enabled by material resources.

This does not invalidate her care.

It prevents the analysis from turning her competence into an entirely individual virtue.

Saori can make rooms available because she possesses rooms.

She can circulate objects because she has access to objects.

She can invite people into otaku culture partly because she inherited an enormous amount of its material infrastructure.

---

# 21\. Saori’s exclusion from Kuroneko’s ideal world

After Volume 8, Saori is angry because Kuroneko’s plans and emotional diagrams centered:

-   Kyousuke;

-   Kirino;

-   and Kuroneko,


while treating Saori as peripheral.

When she asks where she belongs in the ideal-world image, the conversation becomes comic.

But the grievance is serious.

Saori has repeatedly:

-   created the spaces where the others met;

-   maintained the friendships;

-   repaired conflict;

-   and supplied practical resources.


Yet the romance narrative threatens to demote her to facilitator.

This is an implicit critique of heroine-route structure:

> Romantic centrality can erase the social labor that made romance possible.

Saori does not demand to become another romantic claimant.

She demands recognition as a person whose relationship to the group matters.

---

# 22\. Saori stops reading the room

Saori admits that her claim—“I could have handled the crisis better if you had consulted me”—is partly bluff.

She might have failed.

She might have panicked.

But she wanted to be trusted with the burden.

She deliberately stops using her extraordinary social-reading ability and speaks without adapting herself to everyone else’s comfort.

This is growth.

Her skill has often made her disappear into successful mediation. By anticipating what everyone needs, she avoids stating what she needs.

Here she says:

-   I was hurt.

-   I was excluded.

-   I wanted you to rely on me.

-   I am not merely the machine that keeps your friendships running.


Kirino is pleased by Saori’s anger because it signals reduced distance.

---

# 23\. Saori does not discard the mask

The chapter could have ended with Saori removing the swirl glasses and accepting her shy private self as “real.”

It does not.

She puts the glasses back on.

Her conclusion is essentially:

> Every Saori who exists with these friends is truly her.

This is a decisive rejection of the hidden-essence model.

The mask became authentic because:

-   Saori chose it;

-   sustained it;

-   created relationships through it;

-   and allowed it to transform her capacities.


An authored self can become a lived self.

Volume 9 thereby revises the series’ recurring “real versus fake” distinction.

The more useful opposition is not:

-   performed versus natural,


but:

-   performance that permits genuine relation;

-   versus performance used to avoid all relation.


---

# 24\. Otome Road: a female-coded fandom space

Kirino and Sena visit Otome Road in Ikebukuro.

This shifts the cultural geography away from Akihabara’s heavily male-coded consumer landscape.

The chapter emphasizes:

-   BL stores;

-   female recommendation culture;

-   specialized terminology;

-   pairing hierarchies;

-   and the way female fans interpret male relationships through fictional templates.


Sena’s fandom is not simply the female version of Kirino’s eroge collection.

It has its own:

-   ethics of concealment;

-   interpretive habits;

-   social codes;

-   and transformative practices.


## The sisters compare brothers

Kirino and Sena begin boasting about their respective older brothers.

Kirino lists evidence:

-   Kyousuke cycled long distances to obtain her game.

-   He flew to America for her.

-   He opposed her supposed boyfriend.

-   He ended a relationship after she objected.

-   He repeatedly placed her above his own convenience.


Her retelling is exaggerated and self-serving.

That is precisely why it matters.

She is curating a case.

---

# 25\. **「ねぇ……どう思う？」**

After recounting Kyousuke’s behavior, Kirino quietly asks Sena:

> **「ねぇ……どう思う？」**

This is one of the most revealing lines in Volume 9.

She is not simply boasting anymore.

She wants an external verdict.

The implied question may be:

-   Is this ordinary brotherly behavior?

-   Is Kyousuke a siscon?

-   Does he love me in some other way?

-   Am I imagining the significance?

-   What do these actions mean when seen from outside our family?


Kirino cannot ask Kyousuke directly because his answer would define the relationship too dangerously.

She asks another younger sister who also has an unusually devoted brother.

This is Kirino trying to locate her experience within a social category.

---

# 26\. Fandom as displacement—and distortion

Sena cannot provide a straightforward emotional judgment.

Instead, she converts the Kousaka relationship into BL material.

She gender-swaps Kirino into a younger brother figure and creates an eroticized Kyousuke–Kirino pairing.

This serves several functions.

## Displacement

The taboo sister–brother relationship becomes a male–male fictional relationship at a safer distance.

## Interpretation

Sena recognizes domination, dependence, consultation, and erotic charge in Kirino’s story, even if her fannish reading is exaggerated.

## Loss of control

Once transformed into fan material, Kirino’s private testimony becomes:

-   shareable;

-   reproducible;

-   searchable;

-   and effectively impossible to erase.


This mirrors the franchise’s own operation.

*Oreimo* converts the Kousaka siblings’ intimacy into:

-   illustrations;

-   merchandise;

-   alternate scenarios;

-   and audience speculation.


The chapter is self-reflexive about the fact that confessional media can reveal a person while also taking ownership of the revelation away from them.

---

# 27\. Ayase’s interior voice: moral certainty as self-deception

Ayase’s chapter demonstrates that switching narrators does not remove dramatic irony.

Ayase sincerely believes herself to be:

-   morally clear;

-   protective;

-   reasonable;

-   and appropriately suspicious.


Her internal narration continually reveals contradictions.

She initially believes Kyousuke and Kuroneko broke up because of her.

She imagines that his earlier declarations—liking her, wanting to marry her—must have influenced his decision.

When he tells her the breakup had nothing to do with her, she becomes angry.

The reaction exposes the desire beneath the supposed guilt.

> She wanted to believe she mattered enough to change his romantic life.

## Relief disguised as concern

Ayase insists that she is not pleased by the breakup.

Her elevated energy says otherwise.

This does not mean she wants Kyousuke to suffer.

It means that his availability has become emotionally important to her before she can admit why.

---

# 28\. Manami as Ayase’s aspirational older woman

Ayase has begun calling Manami and treating her as a respected elder.

Manami’s voice makes Ayase sit upright despite its softness.

Ayase even jokes about conspiring with Manami to eliminate a “thieving cat,” revealing that she imagines them as occupying related positions around Kyousuke.

Manami immediately rejects the violent fantasy.

Their interaction demonstrates Manami’s quiet authority.

She can:

-   restrain Ayase without humiliating her;

-   deliver difficult information;

-   and redirect emotional chaos through calmness.


This is another example of Manami’s social power being underestimated because it lacks spectacle.

---

# 29\. Ayase misses Kyousuke’s inappropriate attention

Kyousuke apologizes for the flirtatious and sexual comments he previously directed at Ayase and promises to stop.

Ayase says this is what she wanted.

Then she becomes irritated when he actually stops.

She tries:

-   moving closer;

-   smiling;

-   and making herself noticeable,


but receives no exaggerated reaction.

Her internal complaint is that it feels as though she has lost her attractiveness.

This should not be interpreted as retroactive consent to harassment.

Rather, the comedy reveals that the previous behavior also supplied:

-   attention;

-   proof that Kyousuke found her attractive;

-   a predictable relational script;

-   and reassurance that she had unusual power over him.


She wanted the violation to stop without wanting the relationship’s intensity to disappear.

That contradiction is psychologically plausible even though the novel handles it through broad fanservice comedy.

---

# 30\. Ayase revises her view of Kyousuke

Watching him with Kanako, Ayase remembers her first impression:

> Kirino had a kind older brother.

Kyousuke and Kanako interact almost like siblings:

-   insults;

-   commands;

-   practical anticipation;

-   praise;

-   and mutual familiarity.


Ayase sees that Kyousuke’s care is not an erotic performance reserved for attractive girls. It is a recurrent mode of relation.

He becomes useful wherever someone:

-   needs managing;

-   requires encouragement;

-   or behaves too aggressively to ask gently.


This does not eliminate Ayase’s romantic interest.

It helps her understand why the interest developed.

---

# 31\. Kyousuke after the breakup

Ayase notices that Kyousuke seems different.

He retains:

-   kindness;

-   approachability;

-   and humor.


But the careless quality has diminished.

He says that recent events made him realize he must behave more responsibly.

From Ayase’s viewpoint, Volume 8 has visibly changed him even though he narrates his own aftermath more ambiguously.

This is another value of alternate perspective:

> Development that feels uncertain from inside the character may be obvious to observers.

Ayase dislikes the new reserve because it makes him:

-   more mature;

-   less available to her preferred script;

-   and harder to manipulate through indignation.


---

# 32\. The “dark angel’s mistake”

When hot tea spills toward the performers, Kyousuke throws himself over them to protect:

-   the girls;

-   and their costumes.


Ayase sees only the resulting compromising position and attacks him.

She later learns that she has punished an act of protection.

This condenses her entire relationship with Kyousuke.

She repeatedly:

1.  witnesses an ambiguous action;

2.  assumes the worst;

3.  punishes him;

4.  learns that he was helping someone;

5.  feels guilt;

6.  reconstructs moral superiority before vulnerability becomes visible.


Her problem is not merely that she is mistaken.

It is that moral condemnation protects her from confronting how much she cares what sort of person he is.

---

# 33\. Kanako’s first fan

Kanako wants Kyousuke present because he was the first person to tell her she might possess idol talent.

She calls him her first fan.

That acknowledgment matters because Kanako habitually pretends:

-   effort is unnecessary;

-   praise does not affect her;

-   and other people’s opinions are irrelevant.


Her desire to preserve the first validating witness resembles Kirino’s dependence on Kyousuke’s recognition.

Kyousuke often becomes important by seeing a capacity before its owner can safely acknowledge wanting it.

Kanako’s ambition remains independent of him, but his words become part of its origin story.

---

# 34\. ClariS and the franchise entering its own fiction

The chapter includes fictional appearances by Alice and Clara, the performers associated with the anime’s opening theme.

This collapses several layers:

-   the light novel;

-   its anime adaptation;

-   music marketing;

-   real performers;

-   and the fictional *Meruru* event.


The cameo is playful rather than psychologically central, but culturally revealing.

By Volume 9, *Oreimo* is no longer only a novel depicting multimedia otaku culture.

It has become a multimedia franchise capable of folding its own production ecology back into the fiction.

The boundary between:

-   fandom represented by the novel;

-   and fandom surrounding the novel


is becoming porous.

---

# 35\. Kirino’s wedding dress: commercial premise and literary meaning

The afterword is unusually candid about the final story.

Fushimi explains that he wanted to put Kirino in a wedding dress and created the story to fulfill that wish. ÄåüEæ}èGòt)(Æ╟ë┴ìZÉ│12-05-24)-16

This is important evidence against over-purifying the scene.

The wedding imagery is, explicitly:

-   character fanservice;

-   heroine marketing;

-   and an opportunity for Kanzaki Hiro to produce a striking image.


But commercial motivation does not prevent the story from acquiring thematic meaning.

The scene can be both:

-   constructed to sell bridal Kirino;

-   and structurally significant within the sibling relationship.


---

# 36\. Kirino as professional

Kirino’s modeling session runs long, threatening her plan to attend the *Meruru* concert with Kyousuke.

She does not abandon the shoot.

She completes the work.

This parallels Kanako’s professional ethic:

-   private desire matters;

-   but accepting paid work creates responsibility.


Kirino’s public identity is again treated as authentic labor rather than a false shell covering the “real otaku.”

She genuinely values:

-   modeling;

-   professional competence;

-   and the people relying on her.


Her disappointment does not make the work illegitimate.

---

# 37\. Kyousuke restores multiplicity

Kyousuke arrives:

-   bruised;

-   exhausted;

-   still wearing his suit;

-   and riding the Akihabara-themed bicycle.


He intends to get Kirino to the concert despite the delay.

This repeats his central function in her life.

Kirino often believes she must choose:

-   professional work or fandom;

-   athletics or friendship;

-   public identity or hidden identity;

-   ambition or home.


Kyousuke repeatedly creates situations in which she can retain more than one.

He cannot eliminate every contradiction.

He can sometimes refuse the forced choice.

That may be the deepest reason his recognition is uniquely valuable:

> **He does not merely approve of all Kirino’s selves. He actively helps them coexist in time.**

---

# 38\. The anti-fairytale bridal ride

The visual components resemble a romantic escape:

-   a beautiful girl in a wedding dress;

-   a male rescuer;

-   physical closeness;

-   public spectacle;

-   and a journey toward music promising a desired future.


But the execution is aggressively anti-elegant:

-   the dress is torn for mobility;

-   the “vehicle” is a ridiculous pain-bike;

-   Kyousuke is sweaty and injured;

-   Kirino insults him;

-   strangers stare;

-   and the two quarrel continuously.


Kirino reflects:

> **「こういうのが、あたしたちらしいのかも」**

Perhaps this is what they are like.

Their relationship cannot sustain conventional romance imagery without deforming it into:

-   effort;

-   embarrassment;

-   conflict;

-   and comedy.


The result is more personally specific than an idealized bridal scene.

---

# 39\. “Because we are siblings”

During the ride, Kyousuke argues that physical proximity is not sexual because they are siblings.

Kirino replies that his recent use of **兄妹だから** sounds increasingly like an excuse for intimate contact.

This is played as comedy, but it identifies the central category problem.

“Because we are siblings” has previously justified:

-   trust;

-   protection;

-   unrestricted family access;

-   and emotional priority.


It is now being used to neutralize situations visually coded as romantic.

The phrase both:

-   establishes the boundary;

-   and allows them to approach it safely.


As long as contact is called sibling behavior, neither must ask what it would mean under another name.

---

# 40\. The unsaid line

At the concert, noise overwhelms the siblings’ voices.

Kirino tries to say something emotionally difficult.

Kyousuke cannot hear it.

She cannot bring herself to repeat it.

The novel does not reveal the exact words. This withholding should be preserved.

Kirino narrates that expressing feelings is too difficult for her to repeat the line casually.

She therefore substitutes action:

> **「繋いだ手を、しっかりと握りしめる。もう二度とはぐれないように。」**

She grips his hand so that they will never become separated again. ÄåüEæ}èGòt)(Æ╟ë┴ìZÉ│12-05-24)-16

## **はぐれる**

The verb means to:

-   become separated in a crowd;

-   lose track of one another;

-   or stray apart.


It is perfectly literal in the concert setting.

It also gathers the entire series’ history:

-   childhood estrangement;

-   emotional distance;

-   America;

-   Kuroneko’s disappearance;

-   missed invitations;

-   and romantic routes that threaten separation.


Kirino cannot yet say what she wants their relationship to become.

She can say through her body what she does not want:

> She does not want to lose him again.

---

# 41\. The wedding dress does not constitute a confession

The imagery is unmistakably provocative.

Kirino is:

-   dressed as a bride;

-   traveling with Kyousuke;

-   physically attached to him;

-   holding his hand;

-   and making a private promise of continued connection.


But Volume 9 still stops before an explicit romantic choice.

The scene’s emotional content can be fully supported as:

-   sibling loyalty;

-   fear of renewed separation;

-   gratitude;

-   possessiveness;

-   and emerging desire that Kirino cannot categorize.


The bridal image invites the reader to project the ending before the characters have chosen it.

That is both literary foreshadowing and heroine-franchise marketing.

---

# 42\. Japanese voices in Volume 9

## Hinata

Hinata uses a highly colloquial elementary-school voice:

-   あたし;

-   contractions;

-   direct commentary;

-   contemporary slang;

-   and rapid comic judgments.


Her linguistic function is translation.

She converts Kuroneko’s elaborate mythology into ordinary emotional facts.

## Kirino

Kirino’s internal voice resembles her speech—fast, aggressive, slang-heavy—but her narration contains moral reflection rarely audible aloud.

Inside her own perspective, she can admit:

-   guilt;

-   gratitude;

-   admiration;

-   fear;

-   and uncertainty.


The hostility is still present, but it no longer hides everything from the reader.

## Kōhei

Kōhei’s narration is openly enthusiastic and rejects Kyousuke’s negative self-protective rhythm.

He does not arrive at affection through denial.

His exaggerated siscon speech functions as a comic externalization of feelings Kyousuke laboriously suppresses.

## Saori

Saori’s pronoun-switching is the volume’s richest linguistic device.

Her voices are not simply levels of politeness. They are complete social architectures.

The chapter makes grammar itself evidence of plural identity.

## Ayase

Ayase’s narration preserves her polite, morally controlled surface while exposing:

-   jealousy;

-   erotic embarrassment;

-   possessiveness;

-   and self-serving inference.


Her internal voice proves that politeness does not mean emotional composure.

## Kirino’s final silence

The last chapter culminates not in a successful sentence but in a failed one.

Kirino’s voice reaches its limit.

The handclasp becomes the semantic content that speech cannot carry.

---

# 43\. Illustration and paratext

Volume 9’s illustrations reinforce both the polyphonic design and the commercial heroine system.

## Bridal cover imagery

The cover places Kirino in bridal clothing with Kyousuke physically carrying or supporting her.

Before the reader knows the context, the publication presents a wedding-coded sibling image.

The afterword confirms that the visual desire preceded the story.

## Kuroneko at home

The monochrome image of Ruri sprawled with the notebook captures the private, physically exuberant romantic joy that Kyousuke never sees.

It punctures her controlled gothic image and validates Hinata’s testimony.

## The bath and sister ensemble

The Gokō-sister material visually places Kirino inside Kuroneko’s household.

This shifts the heroine triangle into a family environment where Kuroneko has preexisting responsibilities and attachments.

## Saori and Pretty Garden

The illustrations emphasize her history through different appearances and relationships rather than presenting one definitive visual identity.

Her chapter is the visual center of the volume’s plural-self thesis.

## Ayase’s mistake

The compromising image of Kyousuke protecting the performers converts his altruistic action into fanservice comedy from Ayase’s misrecognizing viewpoint.

The reader sees the same ambiguity she sees before receiving the explanation.

## Final hand-in-hand image

Kyousuke and bridal Kirino are shown from behind moving toward the concert together.

The image avoids a face-to-face romantic tableau. It emphasizes:

-   shared direction;

-   movement;

-   and continued connection.


The visual claim is less “they are married” than:

> They are proceeding toward something together.

## Character files

The later character sheets for side figures reinforce Volume 9’s expansion of the franchise ensemble. Characters formerly serving as satellites receive names, designs, family positions, and potential futures of their own.

---

# 44\. Character baselines after Volume 9

## Kyousuke

Kyousuke becomes less authoritative as narrator and more legible as a person observed by others.

Several perspectives independently describe him as:

-   ordinary;

-   kind;

-   dependable;

-   foolish;

-   emotionally vulnerable;

-   and habitually brother-like.


His extraordinary actions are increasingly understood as effort rather than innate heroism.

## Kirino

Kirino now understands Kyousuke as a vulnerable subject rather than an invincible solution-provider.

She also begins actively searching for an interpretation of their bond:

-   through Sena;

-   sibling comparison;

-   bridal imagery;

-   and the unspoken line at the concert.


Her clearest current desire is not yet “be my lover.”

It is:

> Do not become separated from me again.

## Kuroneko

Kuroneko’s love and suffering are externally confirmed.

She remains:

-   a beloved older sister;

-   an independent creator;

-   Kirino’s transformative friend;

-   and someone whose romance with Kyousuke materially affected her household.


Volume 9 protects her from being remembered only as the architect of Volume 8’s manipulation.

## Saori

Saori receives the volume’s most substantial autonomous development.

She is revealed as:

-   a survivor of group dissolution;

-   a composite performance;

-   a social archivist;

-   an inheritor of class and cultural resources;

-   and a person who wants to be relied upon, not merely thanked afterward.


## Ayase

Ayase’s romantic attachment to Kyousuke is now difficult to deny, although she remains unable to call it that.

She wants:

-   his attention;

-   his moral worth;

-   his availability;

-   and proof that she affects him.


Her condemnation and attraction remain tightly interwoven.

## Manami

Manami continues to mediate relationships quietly.

Ayase’s respect for her confirms that her softness carries authority.

She remains one of the few people capable of supplying emotionally useful truth without making herself the center of the scene.

## Kōhei

Kōhei gives the best external diagnosis yet of the Kousaka siblings’ delayed emotional development.

His openness is useful as contrast, though his own siscon behavior remains excessive and ethically imperfect.

## Hinata

Hinata becomes an important interpretive witness.

She sees through Kuroneko’s language, understands family consequences, and recognizes the value of Kirino’s friendship without being absorbed into the romantic triangle.

---

# 45\. Hypothesis status after Volume 9

### Hypothesis 1

**People use socially acceptable, embarrassing, or theatrical identities to disclose what they want.**

**Decisively strengthened.**

Saori’s entire identity is constructed performance that has become relationally authentic.

Volume 9 rejects any simple opposition between mask and truth.

### Hypothesis 2

**Otaku culture is the first confessional space.**

**Confirmed and complicated.**

Fandom permits Kirino to ask indirectly what Kyousuke’s behavior means.

But Sena’s transformation of that testimony into erotic fan material shows that confession can be:

-   distorted;

-   circulated;

-   and appropriated.


Otaku culture creates language without guaranteeing control over interpretation.

### Hypothesis 3

**Kyousuke’s normality is defensive, and he needs to be needed.**

**Revised through external testimony.**

Kyousuke really is ordinary in ability and emotional resilience.

His “heroism” consists of repeatedly exceeding ordinary capacity for particular people.

The need to be needed remains, but Volume 9 emphasizes that others also demand too much from him.

### Hypothesis 4

**Kirino wants one person who accepts every compartment of her identity.**

**Strengthened and refined.**

Kyousuke’s importance comes not only from accepting the compartments but from helping them coexist.

Kirino is now moving from demanding acceptance toward recognizing his own whole personhood.

### Hypothesis 5

**Shared secrecy creates privileged access no outside relationship can reproduce.**

**Still strongly supported, but polyphony limits its exclusivity.**

Other characters understand more than Kyousuke and Kirino realize:

-   Hinata understands Kuroneko.

-   Kōhei understands delayed sibling separation.

-   Saori understands the group.

-   Sena recognizes charged relational material.

-   Manami understands multiple participants.


The sibling bond is unique, but it is not wholly invisible from outside.

### Hypothesis 6

**The heroines represent autonomous alternative futures.**

**Strongly strengthened.**

Volume 9 explicitly grants them narrative subjectivity.

Kuroneko’s family, Saori’s history, and Ayase’s internal contradictions prove that they possess lives exceeding their usefulness as routes.

Yet the volume also reveals the limitation: even their POV chapters frequently remain organized around Kyousuke and Kirino.

### Hypothesis 7

**The ending may grant impossible desire temporary reality while preserving social structure.**

**Further strengthened symbolically, not yet resolved.**

The wedding-dress story rehearses a bridal image while keeping its emotional declaration inaudible.

The franchise visually grants the siblings wedding symbolism before the narrative grants them romantic recognition.

---

# 46\. Critical assessment

Volume 9 is structurally unusual but indispensable.

## Its greatest achievement: correcting protagonist centrality

The volume proves that:

-   Kuroneko’s romance mattered outside Kyousuke’s experience;

-   Saori’s social labor has a history and personal cost;

-   Ayase’s moral policing contains desire;

-   and Kirino’s internal development is more advanced than her dialogue reveals.


Kyousuke remains the central object linking many stories, but he no longer controls their meaning.

## Saori’s chapter is exceptional

*Chameleon Daughter* may be the strongest supporting-character chapter in the series so far.

It turns an exaggerated comic persona into an account of:

-   memory;

-   class;

-   imitation;

-   abandonment;

-   institutional labor;

-   and relational self-creation.


It also directly supports one of the series’ deepest ideas:

> A performed identity may be more rather than less sincere when it is the form through which a person creates the relationships she actually wants.

## The volume retains commercial asymmetry

The afterword admits that Saori’s autonomous story previously had low priority, while the wedding-dress story was created largely from the desire to display Kirino in bridal clothing.

That contrast is revealing.

The franchise’s priorities remain:

-   central-heroine imagery;

-   romantic provocation;

-   attractive costume variants;

-   and multimedia crossover.


Volume 9 creates space for literary depth partly because its “special volume” status loosens the main narrative’s commercial hierarchy.

## Alternate POV does not eliminate heroine consumption

The girls receive interiority, but their perspectives are still marketed through:

-   bath scenes;

-   costumes;

-   romantic embarrassment;

-   and illustrations designed around visual appeal.


The volume critiques reduction while participating in it.

## The final scene is powerful and evasive

Kirino’s handclasp is psychologically appropriate.

She has never been able to express important feelings without:

-   games;

-   insults;

-   requests;

-   performances;

-   or objects.


But the inaudible line also prolongs ambiguity at a commercially useful moment.

The reader receives enough evidence to anticipate romance but not enough to force the characters into a decision.

---

# Volume 9 verdict

Volume 9 does not advance the central romance as dramatically as Volumes 7 and 8.

It does something necessary before that romance can continue:

> It removes Kyousuke’s ownership of the story.

Kuroneko’s love is no longer merely something Kyousuke experienced.

It is something:

-   her sisters witnessed;

-   her family endured;

-   and she carried home in tears.


Saori is no longer merely the funny organizer who makes other people’s relationships possible.

She is:

-   the child left behind by a dissolved community;

-   the inheritor of its objects;

-   and the author of a persona designed to reproduce an entire social world.


Ayase is no longer merely a polite moralist who attacks Kyousuke.

From within, she is a girl who:

-   wants his attention;

-   wants to matter to his choices;

-   and continually mistakes desire for moral outrage.


Kirino is no longer merely the person whose problems Kyousuke solves.

She has begun to recognize:

-   that he is ordinary;

-   that he can be hurt;

-   that using him as a superhero is unfair;

-   and that she must return the care she has received.


The formal principle announced in the prologue is fulfilled:

> **Everyone has a story inaccessible to the narrator who thinks he stands at the center.**

Yet the volume ends by returning to Kirino and Kyousuke.

She finishes professional work, he retrieves her, and the two race through public space in the least elegant bridal image imaginable:

-   torn wedding dress;

-   sweat;

-   injuries;

-   pain-bike;

-   insults;

-   and shared embarrassment.


Kirino thinks this absurdity may be what suits them.

At the concert, she attempts to say something that neither Kyousuke nor the reader is allowed to hear. She cannot repeat it.

Instead, she grips his hand:

> **「もう二度とはぐれないように。」**

Volume 8 established that Kirino must remain first.

Volume 9 gives that demand a more emotionally mature form.

It is no longer only:

> Do not choose another girl over me.

It is becoming:

> See the real me.
> Let me see the real you.
> Do not let us lose each other again.

That movement does not erase the possessiveness or the harm already caused to Kuroneko. It does suggest that Kirino’s desire is developing beyond simple exclusivity into reciprocal recognition.

The wedding dress is deliberate fanservice. The author openly says so.

But the scene’s deeper significance lies in how completely it refuses conventional bridal fantasy.

Kyousuke and Kirino do not appear as an ideal couple proceeding smoothly toward social union.

They appear as two people who:

-   cannot speak clearly;

-   cannot behave normally;

-   cannot stop fighting;

-   and nevertheless keep moving in the same direction while holding on tightly enough not to become separated.


Volume 9 therefore serves as a pause before the final movement, but not a neutral one.

It establishes that the central choice cannot be made responsibly while the surrounding characters remain mere routes or interpretive objects.

Before Kyousuke can choose, the novel must acknowledge:

> **Kuroneko has her own story.
> Saori has her own story.
> Ayase has her own story.
> Kirino has her own story.
> And Kyousuke has understood only portions of all of them—including his sister’s.**

---

**Assistant — Turn 28**

# *Oreimo* Volume 10 Deep Reading

## Leaving home, receiving care, and the heroine who finally names her love

Volume 10 opens with the Kousaka family confronting the possibility that the series has spent nine volumes placing before the reader:

> Have Kyousuke and Kirino become too close to remain intelligible as ordinary siblings?

The evidence assembled by their mother is almost comically damning:

-   Kirino now regularly calls him **京介**;

-   they move freely between each other’s rooms;

-   spend weekends together;

-   returned home holding hands while Kirino wore a wedding dress;

-   and possess a heart-covered purikura depicting them as a couple—which Kyousuke has attached to the family refrigerator.


Both siblings vehemently insist that their relationship is healthy. Their father says that he does not seriously believe Kyousuke has sexually approached Kirino, but recalls the original lie in which Kyousuke claimed ownership of Kirino’s little-sister eroge. The parents therefore impose a temporary separation: Kyousuke must live alone until he earns an A-rank assessment for his preferred university.

This premise initially resembles a social or parental judgment upon the sibling bond. The volume eventually reveals something subtler. Kyousuke’s mother never truly believed incest was occurring. She exploited the possibility—and Kyousuke’s attachment to Kirino—to force her passive son into sustained academic effort.

Volume 10 is therefore not primarily about society separating taboo lovers.

It is about:

-   Kyousuke acting for himself for the first time;

-   the network he built returning the care he once gave;

-   Kirino learning to support rather than merely demand;

-   idealization becoming ethically dangerous when it ignores consent;

-   and Ayase finally transforming moral hostility, gratitude, and dependence into a direct romantic confession.


Its central thesis is:

> **Kyousuke’s independence does not consist of escaping the people who need him. It consists of developing a self capable of choosing a future while accepting that he, too, needs and receives care.**

The volume begins by questioning whether Kyousuke and Kirino are lovers.

It ends with someone else clearly saying that she loves him.

---

# 1\. Narrative structure

Volume 10 consists of five interlocking movements.

## 1\. The family separation

Kyousuke and Kirino’s increasingly intimate behavior leads to a family meeting.

Their father orders Kyousuke to live alone until he earns an A-rank mock-exam result for his preferred university.

Kirino responds:

> **「ずっと帰って来なくてもいいよ」**
> “You never have to come back.”

Her expression and subsequent behavior contradict the words.

## 2\. Kyousuke’s experiment in independent life

Kyousuke moves into a small apartment and begins preparing for the examination.

Friends and family bring him:

-   food;

-   appliances;

-   study materials;

-   furniture;

-   cooking equipment;

-   figures;

-   and emotional support.


The supposed solitary life becomes a physical map of all the relationships established during the series.

## 3\. Domestic competition and Ayase’s caretaking

Kirino’s public and private friends gather for a moving-in party. Several girls compete over who should cook, clean, or otherwise support Kyousuke.

Kirino suppresses the competition because it threatens his studying and assigns Ayase as his temporary caretaker.

Ayase begins visiting daily.

Their interactions increasingly resemble hostile newlywed domesticity.

## 4\. Sayaka and the fan-blog crisis

Kyousuke discovers that an Ayase fan blog has begun condemning Ayase for purchasing a little-sister figure and repeatedly visiting his apartment.

The blogger, Sayaka, is a younger acquaintance who has idealized Ayase and reacts to contradictory evidence through stalking, unauthorized photography, and moral accusation.

The crisis forces Ayase to confront a younger version of her own Volume 2 behavior toward Kirino.

## 5\. Examination, homecoming, and confession

Kyousuke takes the examination despite an injured hand and earns the required A-rank.

He returns to the Kousaka home, where Kirino has colonized his room with her collection.

Afterward, Kyousuke meets Ayase and resolves the foundational lie of their relationship.

Ayase tells him:

> **「そんなあなたのことが好きです」**
> “I love that person—that person who is you.”

---

# 2\. The opening family meeting makes the subtext public

For nine volumes, the romantic resemblance of Kyousuke and Kirino’s relationship has been visible primarily to:

-   readers;

-   heroines;

-   comic rivals;

-   and the siblings themselves in moments they quickly deny.


Volume 10 brings the question into the family home.

Their mother lists evidence not of sexual contact but of **category confusion**.

The siblings now behave in ways that can be read simultaneously as:

-   affectionate family intimacy;

-   heroine-route progression;

-   and romantic couple behavior.


## The evidence is not invented

The mother has observed real developments.

### **京介**

Kirino has moved from impersonal or hostile forms such as:

-   あんた;

-   ねえ;

-   and occasional 兄貴


toward regular use of Kyousuke’s personal name.

The mother calls this a **心境の変化**—a change in emotional state.

The address does not prove romance, but it marks Kyousuke as an individual person rather than merely an irritating family role.

### The wedding-dress homecoming

Kirino’s clothing was professionally motivated, but the siblings did return:

-   dressed like bride and groom;

-   physically exhausted from a joint journey;

-   and holding hands.


### The purikura

The photograph was created during a fake date, but Kyousuke personally attached it to the refrigerator.

The professional excuse cannot explain why he chose to preserve and publicly display the image afterward.

### Mikagami

Kyousuke reacted to a supposed boyfriend with possessiveness far beyond detached concern.

The mother’s suspicion is therefore comically exaggerated but structurally informed.

The series’ central ambiguity has become socially legible.

---

# 3\. The parents do not truly believe in incest

The volume appears to set up parental prohibition as the first direct external obstacle to the sibling route.

It later withdraws that threat.

Kyousuke’s mother explains that she never seriously believed the siblings were sexually involved. She used the appearance of suspicion because she knew that separating Kyousuke from Kirino would activate what she jokingly describes as his **シスコンパワー**.

Her actual goal was academic.

This revelation is important in several ways.

## The family recognizes Kyousuke’s motivation

The parents understand that Kirino has become one of the strongest forces capable of moving their passive son.

They have seen him:

-   confront his father;

-   recover her manuscript;

-   travel overseas;

-   retrieve her from America;

-   and repeatedly exceed his normal effort when her wellbeing is involved.


The mother redirects that same energy toward Kyousuke’s own future.

## The taboo is used rather than confronted

Volume 10 does not yet stage a serious parental reckoning with acknowledged sibling romance.

The suggestion of incest becomes:

-   comic family misunderstanding;

-   an examination pretext;

-   and a mechanism for temporary separation.


This is dramatically effective but ethically evasive.

The family’s eventual acceptance or rejection of an actual romantic relationship remains untested.

## The parents’ manipulation resembles the children’s

The Kousaka family repeatedly communicates indirectly.

-   Kirino disguises requests as insults.

-   Kyousuke disguises love as duty.

-   The mother disguises academic concern as incest suspicion.

-   The father disguises care as authoritarian judgment.


The parents are not fundamentally different from their children. They also create artificial scenarios to produce desired emotional action.

---

# 4\. The father’s trust

The father says that he knows Kyousuke:

-   understands Kirino better than anyone in the household;

-   values her;

-   and has repeatedly acted in her interest.


He does not interpret the original eroge lie literally. He understands that Kyousuke protected Kirino.

Later, he reiterates:

> **「一度決めたことだ、桐乃のことはおまえに任せる」**
> Once he decided to entrust Kirino to Kyousuke, he intends to honor that decision.

This represents the endpoint of the father’s development from Volume 1.

Originally, he believed:

-   Kirino’s interests should be erased;

-   and Kyousuke’s defense represented defiance.


Now he regards Kyousuke as the person most qualified to mediate Kirino’s life.

## Trust becomes delegated authority

This is moving, but also potentially troubling.

The father effectively transfers part of his protective authority over Kirino to her brother.

Kyousuke becomes:

-   confidant;

-   interpreter;

-   protector;

-   and quasi-custodian.


That reinforces the uniqueness of their bond, but risks treating Kirino’s autonomy as something managed by the men who understand or protect her.

The volume does not fully interrogate this paternal structure.

---

# 5\. Separation exposes Kyousuke’s attachment

Kyousuke initially tells himself that temporary solitary life is desirable.

He will gain:

-   privacy;

-   independence;

-   freedom from family conflict;

-   and a preview of university life.


Yet he cannot become excited.

Kirino’s statement—

> **「ずっと帰って来なくてもいいよ」**

—continually returns to him.

As in Volumes 4 and 5, physical separation reveals feelings ordinary proximity lets him deny.

He claims:

-   Kirino annoys him;

-   leaving home is advantageous;

-   and he will return soon anyway.


But the prospect of no longer sharing daily space with her creates an emotional weight he cannot dismiss.

## Home is no longer merely the house

Kyousuke wants to return not because the apartment is materially uncomfortable.

He receives ample:

-   food;

-   appliances;

-   visitors;

-   and parental support.


What he misses is the particular relational structure of the Kousaka home:

-   Kirino behind the next wall;

-   her demands;

-   their exchanges;

-   and his embedded place in the family.


The desire to return is less dependence on domestic service than dependence on continuity with Kirino.

---

# 6\. Mikagami and Akagi: male perspectives on sibling dependence

Kyousuke discusses the separation with Mikagami and Akagi.

The resulting male gathering is comically saturated with abnormal sibling relationships.

## Akagi

Kōhei remains open about his attachment to Sena.

He immediately recognizes that separation from a sister would be the central disadvantage of living alone.

His transparency continues to function as a contrast with Kyousuke’s denial.

## Mikagami

Mikagami assumes that Kyousuke’s distress concerns being separated from his beloved Kirino and jokes about how he might marry her.

His interpretation is exaggerated, but he accurately sees that Kyousuke’s anxiety is not purely academic.

## Mikagami’s older brother

Mikagami lives with an older brother who:

-   pays little;

-   is supported financially by his younger sibling;

-   cooks and occupies space;

-   and appears comfortable with dependency.


The arrangement disturbs Kyousuke.

The anxiety later enters a dream in which an adult Kyousuke:

-   has failed his examinations;

-   remains financially dependent on Kirino;

-   lives with her;

-   cooks for her;

-   and inhabits an eroticized cohabitation scenario.


The dream is not evidence of an unconscious incest wish in a simple psychoanalytic sense. It combines several immediate anxieties:

-   failure;

-   dependence;

-   loss of adult status;

-   cohabitation;

-   and the erotic framing surrounding siblings living alone.


Kyousuke wakes and rejects it as absurd.

But the scenario clarifies what he fears:

> Remaining with Kirino without independently becoming an adult could turn care into parasitism.

Volume 10 therefore makes his academic success relevant to the sibling relationship.

Before he can claim a future with anyone, he must become capable of standing beside rather than beneath them.

---

# 7\. Kyousuke pursues a goal for himself

This is Volume 10’s most important development.

Kyousuke has repeatedly displayed extraordinary effort, but usually on behalf of another person.

He has worked for:

-   Kirino;

-   Kuroneko;

-   Ayase;

-   Manami;

-   Saori;

-   Kanako;

-   and their shared community.


Academic preparation is different.

He says, in effect:

> This time, he is acting for **高坂京介**.

## From ordinary life to chosen effort

Kyousuke’s original ideology treated “ordinary” as permission to avoid:

-   difficult ambitions;

-   measurable failure;

-   and commitment.


Volumes 3 and 4 began breaking that defense by showing him Kirino’s sustained labor.

Volume 10 requires him to act upon the lesson.

He recognizes two truths:

1.  Some things genuinely exceed one’s ability.

2.  Some things become possible only after one stops using inability as a preemptive excuse.


He studies seriously and earns an A-rank assessment.

## His first self-directed victory

Kyousuke regards the result as something new.

It is not:

-   Kirino’s recovered manuscript;

-   Ayase’s reconciliation;

-   Kuroneko’s completed game;

-   or someone else’s restored life.


It is evidence that he can choose and pursue his own future.

That matters for the romantic endgame.

A protagonist who exists only as helper cannot make an ethically meaningful romantic choice. He would merely continue responding to whichever girl presents the most urgent need.

Volume 10 begins constructing a Kyousuke who can act from his own desire.

---

# 8\. Independence is supported, not solitary

Kyousuke’s apartment quickly fills with objects from other people.

He records receiving:

-   a refrigerator from Kirino;

-   study books from Manami;

-   food from Kanako;

-   a toaster promised by Akagi;

-   a knife from Ayase;

-   two meals from Kuroneko;

-   and repeated further assistance.


His father sees the overflowing acquisition log and reacts as though Kyousuke has turned solitary living into a life of female patronage.

The comedy reveals the volume’s more serious point:

> Kyousuke’s capacity to live independently has been socially produced.

He succeeds because he has created relationships in which care circulates back toward him.

## Care as return rather than repayment

Ayase later explains:

> **「お兄さんからもらったものを、お返ししているだけなんですから」**

The girls are returning things they received from him.

This is not literal debt accounting.

Kyousuke gave them:

-   attention;

-   advocacy;

-   mediation;

-   courage;

-   and practical help.


They return:

-   food;

-   domestic labor;

-   materials;

-   advice;

-   and presence.


The volume revises the rescuer structure.

Kyousuke is no longer the sole active party surrounded by girls who receive his solutions.

He becomes the recipient of a network of accumulated gratitude.

---

# 9\. The apartment becomes the route-selection room

Nearly every heroine enters Kyousuke’s independent living space and offers a form of domestic support.

This converts the apartment into a parody of the heroine-route system.

## Manami

She brings:

-   study materials;

-   food;

-   and educational discipline.


Her support is the most conventional extension of their existing relationship.

## Kuroneko

She brings handmade cutlet sandwiches and renewed route tension.

Her care is intimate but restrained by the agreement that Kyousuke will not date anyone until resolving the Kirino question.

## Ayase

She supplies:

-   cooking;

-   cleaning;

-   a knife;

-   daily visits;

-   and increasingly explicit domestic intimacy.


## Kanako

She brings food and tests whether Kyousuke will recognize her effort.

## Kirino

She buys the refrigerator, supplies eroge, and places the entire trial inside a wager.

## Saori

She helps create and coordinate the ensemble social environment.

The volume packages each heroine as a plausible domestic future while refusing to make Kyousuke choose among them.

The most substantial route belongs to Ayase, but the structure remains openly competitive.

---

# 10\. Kirino’s refrigerator

Kirino buys Kyousuke a refrigerator because she expects him to survive on convenience-store food.

The gift embodies her characteristic care:

-   expensive;

-   practical;

-   intrusive;

-   and disguised by insults.


She refuses to say simply:

> I worry that you will not eat properly.

Instead, she chooses an object that will continue functioning in her absence.

## Kirino has learned from Kyousuke’s style of care

Kyousuke repeatedly expresses affection through:

-   acquiring objects;

-   arranging environments;

-   solving logistical problems;

-   and protecting possibilities.


Kirino now does the same.

She cannot remain at his apartment.

She can make it easier for him to live there.

The refrigerator becomes her practical substitute.

---

# 11\. The “little-sister wife” game

Kirino also gives Kyousuke:

> 『おしかけ妹妻～禁断の二人暮りし～』

The game’s premise—an eroticized younger sister sharing domestic life with her brother—directly mirrors the taboo anxiety that caused Kyousuke’s separation.

Kirino says it is for stress relief.

Possible interpretations coexist:

-   she genuinely treats the genre as ordinary hobby material;

-   she enjoys provoking him;

-   she is testing his reaction to sibling cohabitation;

-   she is asserting symbolic presence inside his apartment;

-   or she is unconsciously reproducing the romanticized domestic fantasy the family has just prohibited.


The novel does not choose one definitive motive.

The important fact is that Kirino responds to forced separation by placing a fictional little sister in Kyousuke’s new home.

As in Volume 5, eroge becomes her proxy.

---

# 12\. Kirino’s wager

Kirino proposes:

-   if Kyousuke fails, he becomes her slave;

-   if he earns the A-rank, she will grant one request.


This resembles:

-   a game condition;

-   a route reward;

-   and another renamed life consultation.


The wager converts academic effort into relational play.

## Her stated and actual incentives conflict

On the surface, Kirino benefits if he fails.

In practice, she:

-   buys equipment;

-   monitors progress;

-   stops distractions;

-   selects a caretaker;

-   reads the father’s reports;

-   challenges insufficient effort;

-   and waits for him at the examination site.


She wants to lose the bet.

More precisely, she wants Kyousuke to prove that he can win.

Her hostility is motivational theater.

## **本気**

Kirino tells him:

> **「家に帰りたいって思うなら、ちゃんと本気出しなさいよ」**

If he truly wants to come home, he must become serious.

Kirino’s central moral language has always involved total effort.

She does not comfort Kyousuke by saying:

-   the standard is unfair;

-   his current level is sufficient;

-   or failure would change nothing.


She says:

> Act as though your stated desire matters.

This can be harsh, but it is precisely the challenge Kyousuke requires.

---

# 13\. Kirino stops the domestic competition

At the moving-in party, the girls begin arguing over who should:

-   prepare meals;

-   visit;

-   supervise;

-   and care for Kyousuke.


The scene is comic harem material, but Kirino responds with genuine urgency:

> **「京介の邪魔すんなぁっ！」**

Do not interfere with Kyousuke.

Her intervention is important because she does not claim the domestic role for herself.

Instead, she prioritizes:

-   his examination;

-   his future;

-   and his return home


over heroine competition.

## Care without monopolizing the task

Kirino could insist that only she understands him.

She does not.

She evaluates who can help effectively.

She excludes:

-   Manami, partly because of their unresolved history;

-   Kuroneko, because of romantic complication;

-   Kanako, because she is unreliable;

-   and Saori, because her intervention would likely enlarge the social event.


She selects Ayase.

This decision demonstrates practical trust in another person’s competence.

It also creates the volume’s major romantic route.

Kirino supports Ayase’s proximity to Kyousuke even though Volume 8 established that she cannot tolerate him having a girlfriend.

The contradiction may arise because Kirino believes Ayase is safe:

-   Ayase claims to hate him;

-   acts morally severe;

-   and has not yet confessed romantic interest.


Kirino miscalculates.

---

# 14\. Manami and the deferred reckoning

Manami asks Kirino for a serious conversation.

Kirino agrees—but only after the examination.

She explains that:

-   the subject concerns Kyousuke;

-   it may affect him;

-   and she will not permit the confrontation to disrupt his preparation.


This suggests that Manami possesses information or judgment central to the sibling relationship.

The conversation itself is deferred to Volume 11.

## Kirino exercises restraint

Earlier Kirino would likely have responded to Manami with:

-   immediate hostility;

-   territorial aggression;

-   or avoidance.


Here she controls the timing.

She acknowledges that:

-   Manami has a legitimate claim to speak;

-   the issue cannot be permanently avoided;

-   but Kyousuke’s immediate future takes priority.


## Manami sees through the act

Manami describes Kirino as caring deeply about her brother.

Kirino rejects the wording, but her actions confirm it.

Manami remains the person most capable of identifying the sibling relationship without accepting the siblings’ own evasive labels.

---

# 15\. Ayase as domestic partner

Ayase arrives at Kyousuke’s apartment at six in the morning with a copied key and a kitchen knife as a housewarming present.

The imagery combines:

-   newlywed domesticity;

-   home invasion;

-   threat;

-   and comedy.


This is Ayase’s relational grammar in miniature.

She provides genuine care while protecting herself through hostility.

## Daily visits

Ayase:

-   cooks;

-   cleans;

-   manages household tasks;

-   checks Kyousuke’s condition;

-   and keeps him oriented toward study.


She admits that she has deliberately made time to visit every day.

The practical arrangement begins resembling cohabitation.

When Kyousuke uses the phrase **共同生活**, Ayase observes that it sounds like lovers living together.

The volume openly places her in the domestic-girlfriend role.

## Care as returned gift

Ayase says she is not performing charity.

She is returning what Kyousuke gave her.

He helped her:

-   reconcile with Kirino;

-   recognize her own idealization;

-   navigate fandom she disliked;

-   and repeatedly resolve problems through consultation.


The domestic labor is her attempt to make gratitude concrete.

---

# 16\. Ayase enjoys the hostile script

Kyousuke and Ayase repeatedly return to their familiar pattern:

-   he says something sexually inappropriate;

-   she condemns him;

-   he exaggerates;

-   she threatens violence;

-   and both continue engaging.


Volume 9 showed that Ayase became unsettled when Kyousuke stopped directing inappropriate attention toward her.

Volume 10 demonstrates why.

Their hostile script provides:

-   predictable intimacy;

-   proof that he notices her;

-   a reason to remain near him;

-   and emotional intensity that can be called moral correction rather than flirtation.


## The script is ethically compromised

Kyousuke’s harassment should not be retroactively declared harmless merely because Ayase later loves him.

Much of it was unwanted, humiliating, or coercive in context.

The novels use the behavior as comedy and romantic rhythm, but psychological reinterpretation does not erase its boundary violations.

Ayase’s eventual confession means:

-   affection grew inside the relationship;

-   not that every previous action was secretly acceptable.


---

# 17\. Ayase investigates Kirino’s hobby

Ayase begins asking Kyousuke about little-sister eroge and purchases a related figure.

She does not suddenly become an enthusiast.

Her actions represent an attempt to understand a part of Kirino she once rejected.

This extends the compromise from Volume 2.

Ayase has moved from:

-   condemnation;

-   to reluctant coexistence;

-   to active curiosity.


She still finds the material objectionable.

But she now believes that love for Kirino creates an obligation to understand why it matters.

## The figure becomes socially dangerous

Sayaka photographs Ayase purchasing the figure.

An object chosen as an act of friendship is reinterpreted as evidence of moral corruption.

This repeats Kirino’s original exposure:

-   the object is real;

-   the external interpretation is reductive;

-   and the person’s entire identity is threatened by one stigmatized attachment.


Ayase is now placed in Kirino’s former position.

---

# 18\. Sayaka: Ayase’s mirror

Sayaka is a younger girl who admires Ayase as:

-   beautiful;

-   morally pure;

-   accomplished;

-   kind;

-   and ideal.


When evidence contradicts this image, Sayaka does not revise her understanding.

She concludes that Ayase has betrayed the “real Ayase.”

This directly mirrors Ayase’s Volume 2 response to Kirino.

## The same real/fake division

Sayaka believes:

-   ideal Ayase = real;

-   figure-buying, apartment-visiting Ayase = corrupted or false.


Ayase recognizes the pattern and tells her:

> **「あなたの大好きな『あやせ先輩』も『あやせちゃん』も、あなたの中にしかいないんですよ」**

The ideal Ayase exists only in Sayaka’s mind.

This is not a claim that Sayaka’s admiration was entirely imaginary.

Ayase really is:

-   beautiful;

-   accomplished;

-   and often kind.


The error lies in treating a partial image as total ownership.

## Ayase has become capable of self-application

In Volume 2, Kyousuke had to show Ayase that she had replaced Kirino with an idealized version.

Now Ayase recognizes the same mistake in another person.

She has internalized the lesson.

Her development is not that she has stopped idealizing people altogether. It is that she can identify the mechanism and refuse to participate in someone else’s purification fantasy.

---

# 19\. Fandom, photography, and consent

Sayaka’s photography is not dismissed as worthless.

She:

-   saved money for equipment;

-   learned technical skill;

-   built a blog;

-   cultivated aesthetic attention;

-   and genuinely loves photographing Ayase.


The problem is what she does with the attachment:

-   covert surveillance;

-   unauthorized publication;

-   moral policing;

-   threats;

-   and possession of someone else’s image.


Kyousuke distinguishes hobby from conduct.

He tells her, in effect:

> Her photography may be meaningful.
> Her violation of Ayase is still wrong.

This is an important development of the series’ ethics.

## Earlier formulation

Volumes 1 and 2 argued:

-   unusual taste does not make someone criminal;

-   fictional desire is not action;

-   and a person should not be reduced to a stigmatized hobby.


## Volume 10 formulation

Volume 10 adds:

-   actions require consent;

-   affection does not grant ownership;

-   harmful conduct must be stopped;

-   and defending someone’s humanity does not require approving everything they do.


Kyousuke says:

> **「気持ち悪くない趣味なんかないよ」**

And:

> **「キモかろうが、悪かろうが。バカにしていい趣味なんかない」**

Bad actions may deserve punishment, and repellent material may be rejected, but contempt for the person and the attachment should not become automatic.

This is the most developed form of the series’ acceptance ethic so far.

---

# 20\. Kyousuke is implicated in the violation

The moral resolution is not entirely clean.

Kyousuke had been:

-   bookmarking Sayaka’s blog;

-   routinely saving unauthorized photographs of Ayase;

-   and enjoying the material without asking how it was obtained.


He becomes concerned only when the blog turns threatening and personally relevant.

This weakens his moral authority.

He criticizes Sayaka for:

-   stalking;

-   covert photography;

-   and unauthorized publication,


but does not fully confront his own role as the consumer who rewarded the system.

The volume could have made a stronger point:

> Violative fan practices are sustained not only by the obsessive creator but by audiences willing to consume decontextualized images.

Instead, Kyousuke’s complicity is mostly absorbed into comedy.

This is one of the volume’s largest ethical blind spots.

---

# 21\. Kyousuke’s mediation remains distinctive

Despite that failure, his handling of Sayaka reflects real growth.

He does not divide her into:

-   innocent fan;

-   or irredeemable stalker.


He can simultaneously say:

-   what you did was wrong;

-   apologize;

-   stop violating Ayase;

-   your photographs have value;

-   your dream does not need to be discarded;

-   and you must ask permission.


This resembles his approach to Kirino.

He does not solve contradiction by amputating one side.

He seeks a form in which:

-   desire survives;

-   and conduct changes.


That is the deepest continuity in Kyousuke’s ethics.

---

# 22\. Ayase and the foundational lie

Volume 2’s reconciliation depended partly on Kyousuke’s false incest confession.

He claimed that:

-   the eroge belonged to him;

-   he and Kirino shared forbidden love;

-   and he was the dangerous influence from whom Ayase should rescue her friend.


Ayase later reveals that she had understood the claim was false.

## Why she accepted the deception

The lie gave her a structure in which she could:

-   continue loving Kirino;

-   avoid immediately accepting the hobby;

-   and transfer disgust and blame onto Kyousuke.


Kyousuke became the villain who made coexistence possible.

This is morally unfair to him, but psychologically functional.

Ayase could preserve:

-   her image of Kirino;

-   her moral objections;

-   and the friendship


by allowing Kyousuke to carry the contradiction.

## A useful lie

Ayase hates dishonesty.

Yet she eventually says:

> **「わたし、嘘は大嫌いですけど。こんな嘘も……あるんですね」**

Some lies can create time or space in which people eventually become capable of a truth they could not initially bear.

This does not make lies inherently good.

Kyousuke’s deception delayed:

-   honest understanding;

-   accountability;

-   and a more mature relationship among all three.


But it also prevented an immediate rupture that might have become permanent.

The volume’s position is pragmatic:

> A false story may temporarily protect a real relationship, but it eventually has to be acknowledged as false.

---

# 23\. The apology arrives one year late

Kyousuke and Ayase finally have the conversation that could not occur in Volume 2.

Kyousuke apologizes for deceiving her.

Ayase apologizes for:

-   using him as a convenient villain;

-   treating him cruelly;

-   and permitting him to absorb blame that did not belong to him.


This is another instance of **returned care**.

Ayase no longer merely receives the benefit of his lie.

She recognizes its cost to him.

The relationship becomes more reciprocal at exactly the moment she is prepared to confess.

---

# 24\. Ayase’s confession

Ayase’s confession is built from insults.

She calls Kyousuke:

-   a liar;

-   pervert;

-   siscon;

-   lolicon;

-   masochist;

-   meddler;

-   fool;

-   irrational person;

-   kind person;

-   and someone who continually confuses her.


Then:

> **「そんなあなたのことが好きです」**

The demonstrative **そんな** matters.

She does not say:

> I love you despite all those traits.

She says:

> I love the person constituted by all of them.

This closely parallels the integrated-identity thesis applied to Kirino.

Kyousuke once argued:

> All of those contradictory parts are Kirino.

Ayase now says:

> All of those contradictory parts are the Kyousuke she loves.

## Ayase loves the whole irritating person

Her confession is not based on purification.

Kyousuke does not become:

-   polite;

-   sexually restrained;

-   socially elegant;

-   or morally uncomplicated.


Ayase’s love arises through conflict with the person who repeatedly:

-   violates her expectations;

-   frustrates her moral categories;

-   and nevertheless acts kindly.


This makes her confession psychologically coherent.

---

# 25\. Ayase’s route is not merely gratitude

Gratitude is central, but it does not fully explain her feeling.

Ayase is attracted to several features of Kyousuke’s relational style.

## He refuses idealization

He does not treat Ayase only as:

-   perfect beauty;

-   model;

-   moral authority;

-   or Kirino’s ideal friend.


He sees:

-   aggression;

-   jealousy;

-   insecurity;

-   repression;

-   and absurdity.


Sayaka loves an image.

Kyousuke remains attached to the difficult person.

## He survives her hostility

Ayase’s aggression tests whether someone will:

-   withdraw;

-   condemn her;

-   or remain.


Kyousuke repeatedly remains.

## He gives her a role outside perfection

With Kyousuke, Ayase can be:

-   furious;

-   frightening;

-   embarrassed;

-   unreasonable;

-   and comic.


She does not need to maintain the polished identity Sayaka worships.

## He allows moral conflict to remain

Kyousuke does not force Ayase to like Kirino’s media.

He makes it possible for her to preserve friendship without false agreement.

Her love therefore grows from the same principle that defined Volume 2:

> intimacy can survive unresolved contradiction.

---

# 26\. Ayase’s directness compared with Kirino

Ayase has spent much of the series speaking through:

-   moral condemnation;

-   violence;

-   suspicion;

-   and hostile politeness.


At the climax she says exactly what she feels.

Like Kuroneko, she eventually crosses the threshold Kirino cannot.

Ayase’s route therefore presents another socially possible romance founded on substantial history.

Kyousuke is once again confronted by a girl who is willing to say what his sister cannot.

---

# 27\. Kuroneko and Ayase become rival-allies

Kuroneko and Ayase meet repeatedly around Kyousuke’s apartment.

Their exchanges remain hostile, but the nature of the hostility changes.

Kuroneko eventually identifies Ayase as:

-   a powerful enemy standing before her fate;

-   and a companion aiming toward the same place.


This is a striking formulation.

They are rivals because both want Kyousuke.

They are allies because both want:

-   clarity;

-   ethical resolution;

-   and an end to the sibling ambiguity preventing any route from proceeding cleanly.


## The heroines share a structural enemy

The enemy is not simply Kirino.

It is the unresolved system in which:

-   Kyousuke refuses to choose;

-   Kirino demands priority without naming romance;

-   and every outside relationship remains provisional.


Kuroneko and Ayase occupy different routes but face the same condition.

Their alliance makes the heroine economy increasingly self-aware.

---

# 28\. Kuroneko’s route remains active

Kyousuke tells Kuroneko that he will not date anyone until resolving things with Kirino.

This is the Volume 8 pact stated directly to the former girlfriend.

Kuroneko responds as though events remain according to plan.

She has not:

-   renounced him;

-   accepted permanent defeat;

-   or withdrawn from the group.


Her position is still:

> Once the truth is known, Kyousuke must choose.

Ayase’s confession therefore does not replace Kuroneko’s route.

It adds another fully articulated claim to the endgame.

---

# 29\. Kanako’s developing attachment

Kanako’s contribution is lighter but not meaningless.

She calls Kyousuke because she wants him to act as her manager again.

Her reason is that he treats her differently from professional adults:

-   he does not flatter;

-   defer;

-   or reduce her to talent.


He engages her as an equal nuisance.

## Cooking as experiment

Kanako has begun learning to cook from Manami.

This effort connects to:

-   family tension;

-   desire to reconcile with an ill parent;

-   and experimentation with domestic care.


The food she gives Kyousuke is partly flirtation and partly a test of competence.

## Acceptance of Kirino

Kanako eventually learns that Kyousuke is Kirino’s brother but still does not know Kirino is an otaku.

When asked how she would react, Kanako says she would tease Kirino rather than reject her.

Kyousuke asks Kanako to remain Kirino’s friend.

This reflects his continuing role as guardian of Kirino’s integrated life, even while physically separated from her.

---

# 30\. One-person living does not remove Kyousuke from Kirino’s orbit

The apartment ostensibly creates distance.

Instead, Kirino becomes present everywhere through:

-   the refrigerator;

-   the eroge;

-   the examination wager;

-   phone calls;

-   progress reports;

-   visits;

-   and the promise of returning home.


Separation makes the relationship more visible rather than less.

Kyousuke’s new independence is motivated by:

-   returning to the family;

-   fulfilling Kirino’s challenge;

-   and proving himself capable of the future.


The volume therefore does not show him escaping her emotional gravity.

It shows him using that gravity to move.

---

# 31\. The examination-day injury

Sayaka’s attack causes Kyousuke to fall and injure his hand shortly before the examination.

The injury creates a familiar heroic possibility:

-   Kyousuke sacrifices his own goal while solving someone else’s crisis.


For much of the series, that would have been his default resolution.

Volume 10 refuses it.

He still mediates the Sayaka conflict, but then proceeds to the examination.

## Helping another person no longer requires self-erasure

This is meaningful progress.

Kyousuke does not abandon:

-   Ayase;

-   Sayaka;

-   or his ethical intervention.


But he also does not treat his own future as expendable.

He carries both responsibilities.

That is the adult form of the integrated-self principle.

Just as Kirino refuses to choose between:

-   athletics;

-   friendship;

-   fandom;

-   and family,


Kyousuke begins refusing the choice between:

-   care for others;

-   and care for himself.


---

# 32\. Kirino waits for him

Kirino is present at the examination site and later helps take him to the hospital.

Her response to his effort is understated:

> **「お疲れ」**

The phrase means:

-   good work;

-   you must be tired;

-   I saw what you did;

-   and the task is over.


Kirino does not deliver a sentimental speech.

She recognizes the labor.

That is precisely what Kyousuke learned to do for her in Volume 3.

Their reciprocity now includes not only rescuing one another but witnessing effort.

---

# 33\. Homecoming

After receiving the A-rank, Kyousuke returns to the family home.

Kirino says:

> **「おかえり」**

The phrase continues its accumulated role in the series.

It means more than physical entry.

It confirms:

-   the separation was temporary;

-   the shared home remains his place;

-   and Kirino wanted him back despite saying otherwise.


The beginning and ending form a clear contrast:

### Beginning

> **「ずっと帰って来なくてもいいよ」**

### Ending

> **「おかえり」**

Kirino’s true desire is communicated through the final greeting rather than the earlier insult.

---

# 34\. The “little-sister kingdom”

Kyousuke discovers that Kirino has transformed his bedroom into an **妹大国**:

-   games;

-   merchandise;

-   decorative flags;

-   curtains;

-   and her collection occupy the space.


The scene is comic revenge and fanservice.

It also has deeper symbolic significance.

## Kirino’s hidden world enters Kyousuke’s room

In Volume 1, Kirino’s collection existed in a concealed compartment of her own room.

Kyousuke entered as a cautious outsider.

By Volume 10, the collection has crossed the household and colonized his private space.

Her once-hidden identity no longer remains compartmentalized.

It has become part of his everyday environment.

## Integration becomes invasion

The symbolism is not purely positive.

Kirino modifies Kyousuke’s room without consent.

Her identity is integrated by overwhelming his.

This reflects the broader relational danger:

> Kyousuke has created room for the whole Kirino, but Kirino’s need for priority may leave insufficient room for Kyousuke’s separate self.

Volume 10’s academic arc counters this by giving him an achievement belonging to himself.

The bedroom ending humorously places the two developments in conflict:

-   Kyousuke has become more independent.
